One place where type narrowing is incredibly useful is when you're dealing with function overloads. And a function overload is essentially when you have multiple different function signatures for calling a single function. This is really common in like Node.js, you'll see this all the time, but oftentimes I don't find myself writing overload functions very often, and you probably don't either, but it is something you'll probably run into at least in your TypeScript career a couple times, so it's important to understand how it works. So in our case, we have a sum function.

And the sum function works in two different ways. In the first option, I can pass it an array of values and it'll sum up all of those values together for me. And in the second option, I will pass it two separate numbers and it'll sum those two numbers for me. So I have two different ways that I can call this function. So you may think of writing the function similarly to this. So I can have a function sum, I have an option A and B, and then it's going to do some code inside of here. And option A, we know can either be an array.

or it can be a number. So we know that this is going to be a number or it's going to be an array of numbers. And option B we know is optional and we know it'll be a number. This may be how you think about writing this function signature. But this isn't quite what we expect because as you can see, I can actually pass a number to here as well. And that's not how sum should work. If I pass it just one argument, it's an array. But if I pass it two arguments, they should both be numbers. So the fact that I can write this code right here

Is incorrect. So instead, this is where you need to have a function overload, where if you want multiple different signatures for your function, then you need to define each of them individually and then write one function that handles all of them. So what we need to do directly above this sum function right here, we need to write our different overloaded versions of this to narrow down the different ways we can call this. And I'll leave in this third option here, this incorrect one. Put it down here. This should have an error on it if we've done everything correctly.

So let's do the simplest case first where we just pass in an array of numbers. So we're gonna say function sum, this is going to be our numbers, and it's going to be a number array, just like that. By doing this, we've essentially defined our first overload. We just need to make sure we give it a return type, because when you define overloads, you must give them all return types. It has no implementation, it just is a function that has all the parameters and the return type defined, but no implementation at all.

So right now we have one implementation of this function done, which takes in an array. So as you can see, S1 has no errors. These other ones have errors because we only expect one parameter. Now this project, instead of writing out your own code, we're going to be taking an existing project that's written in JavaScript. As you can see, it's a mind sweeper game over here. If you left click, it's going to reveal a tile, while right clicking it will mark it in this yellow color to represent a mine. So a pretty simple mind sweeper implementation. And what we're going to do is we're going to convert it from JavaScript to TypeScript. I'm going to be talking about a lot of different things you should focus on when you're converting from JavaScript to TypeScript.

Now in this introduction video, before I let you loose on this project, I'll mention two different ways that you can convert something from JavaScript to TypeScript. The first way that you can convert something from JavaScript to TypeScript is with the top-down approach. This is really great if you're only converting parts of your application over to TypeScript at a time. Like maybe you want to convert the most important parts of your application to TypeScript first, and you're going to leave some of the less important parts till later. So a top-down approach means you're going to focus on the top level, such as a feature, like I'm going to be focusing on checkout.

And you're going to change everything in your checkout code to make it work in TypeScript. And then you're just going to trickle that down until you get all of the code all the way down to the smallest functions that are used in that checkout code, all typed with everything you need. So you're working from the top level feature and going down, adding types to all of the different smaller things that that depends on. The other approach is the bottom up approach where you're focusing on the smallest pieces of your entire code base. So like individual functions that have very few things that depend on them.

In this particular example, if I scroll down to the bottom of the file, you can see there's certain functions here that have no dependencies and they're very simple. So you would be starting with these particular functions first and working your way up, adding more and more types as you go. Now there's pros and cons to both. The pro of the top-down approach is that you're able to get one particular part of your application, like one feature, typed relatively quickly since you're only focusing on that one thing.

The con to it is that it can become a mess very quickly because as you start to change one thing, it depends on 10 other things, which you all need to change. And those 10 things all depend on six other things. So now you have 60 different things that you need to change and it quickly spirals out of control. So there's a lot of extra things you need to change in the top-down approach. Well, with the bottom-up approach, everything is incredibly small. And as you start moving up to the things that depend on other things, you're going to notice it's much easier to type those because you've already typed most of the things that they depend on.

Sneha Mehra (00:02:18)

So what I would do in this case is I would go to the Veat documentation and look at how TypeScript works within Veat, because there may be specific things you need to know. Another thing you can do is to create a project from scratch using this bundler, such as VIT.

And they have a TypeScript option. So if your bundler has an option for TypeScript built in, create a project from scratch, look at how they have their code configured there, and you can use that configuration directly into your code as well. So that's kind of like two different ways you can solve this problem. Now, if you're not using a bundler in your project, what you pretty much need to do is consider okay, do I want to move all my code over to a bundler? Which the answer is probably yes, or do I want to manually write all of my TypeScript code myself?

So you kind of have to look at that case by case, but generally you're using some type of bundler. And if you are, you can look at the documentation to figure out how to implement TypeScript into that bundler. So that's what I would recommend doing on this project when you're trying to implement this in Vit. Just look at how VIT does TypeScript and try to move that into this project as well.

One of the more powerful features that you're going to use in TypeScript that I absolutely love is called the Discriminated Union, or you may hear it called the Discriminating Union. And essentially it's a union that allows you to determine which part of the union you're in based on some specific field or flag. To figure out why you want this and what this is, let's take a quick look at this code for a second. We have a type which is a user API response. This response can either be a success or an error, and in the case that we have an error,

We're going to populate this error message with a message. And in the case that we have a success, we're going to populate this data with an ID and a name for the user that we just tried to query for. Now, in order to make sure our type works fine in both cases, we need to make sure our data is optional and our error message is optional, because when our status is error, we're not going to have any data at all. And when our status is success, we're not going to have any error message at all. But I know for a fact that when my status is successful, I will always have data. And when my status is an error,

I will always have an error message. So this leads to some interesting code because as I have this written, data and error message are always optional no matter what. So even when I check for a successful response, I'm getting an error in TypeScript because according to my type, my data could be undefined, even though I know for a fact if I have a successful status, my data should be there. Same thing with my error message. I know if I have an unsuccessful status, I know my error message should be there. So the way we get around this problem.

is by using a discriminated union. The best way to think about that is breaking our response into all the different permutations we know are possible. So we know the error status is possible and the success status is possible. So let's create two different types of responses. The first one is going to be a success response. And in that case, if we look at our code, I know my status here is going to be success. So I know it's just going to be the hard coded string of success. And then I know that I'm going to have my data. So I'll copy this over but I'm not going to make an optional.

So if I have a successful status, I know that my data is going to be there. Now let's copy this down. This is going to be for our error response. Our status is going to be an error every single time, no matter what. And instead of having a data, we're going to have an error message. And this will no longer be optional. So now I've created two different types. One for what happens when I have a successful response and one for what happens when I have an error response. So instead of trying to combine these together into one complicated thing that has a bunch of optional parameters.

Sneha Mehra (00:02:24)  
I've broken them out into two specific different types. Then what I can do is for my overall general type, I know I'm going to be in one of these two scenarios, so I can just use a union. So I can say that we have our success response, or we're gonna have our error response just like that. And now as you can see, this is just a combination of one of those two. And also by just doing that, you'll notice immediately all these errors we had in this function have gone away. And that's because of the power of these discriminated unions.

So the way you can know that you're using a discriminated union is generally you're going to have some type of field which is different between all of your different types. So in our case, our status field is different. So if we have a status of success, we will always be in this particular type. If our status is error, we will always be in this particular type. You'll very often see this with like different key values, for example, for like statuses or something like that. So now if we check for a status of success.

We know that the type of our data here for our response is always going to be a successful response. You can see it has changed the type to a success response because that is the only one of our keys in this union that has a successful status. While down here where we don't have a successful status, you can see our response is set to an error response. Because of our two options, this one is the only one that does not have a status of success. And now my data is automatically a required field. So I know it's always going to be there and I don't have to do any weird if checks to determine if it's there.

Same thing with my error message down here. Now the important thing to understand about this is you can only use literal values when you're trying to do a discriminated union. So if I were to try to say that this is a string and my status down here is a number, this will no longer work. Even if I do this like type of my status is equal to string, and if I spell all of that properly, you'll notice it still doesn't actually discriminatedly unionize this.

for me, and that's because it only works with literal values. So the fact that these are strings versus numbers, that doesn't work. It only works with those literal values of like strings, numbers, and so on. So that's perfectly okay because most places that you want to use a discriminated union, you're going to have different things, such as a status or some other key where you have a bunch of different types that this thing can be. Now the next thing I want to talk about is going to be kind of similar to enums and as const, but this is the idea of tuples. And tuples is just taking an array of values, but saying that this array of values is going to be specifically the first value is one type, the second value is another type, third value is another type, and so on. So instead of just saying I have an array of numbers, I say I have an array where the first index is a number, the second is a string, the third is a number, and so on and so on. The perfect example of this being used in actual JavaScript code.

Is the object.entries method. This method right here actually returns to you a tuple. And a tuple is just saying that it's returning an array because when you call object.entries, it gives you an array. And this array is actually an array of arrays where each individual element array has the first value being the key of the object, so it's going to be a string, and the second value is the value of that key. So in our case, it'll be either a string or a number. And if we hover over our key, you can see it has a type here of string.

And if we hover over our value, you can see it has a type of string or number. That's just because we have strings or numbers as our values. Now the way we would define this ourselves is relatively straightforward. So let's just come in here. I'm going to create a type here. And this type is just going to be called tuple. And we don't even need this to be an object. Instead, I'm going to use an array here. And whatever I want to put in this array is going to be the first value. So let's say the very first value I want to have a string, and the second value in my array I want to be a Boolean. This is a tuple with two values. The first is a string, the second is a Boolean.

So now if I create a variable and I set it to that tuple type, what I need to make sure I do is that the first value of my array must be a string and the second value must be a Boolean. And I can't have any other values on top of that. If I add more values, you can see it gives me an error because this expects there to be only two elements, not three. Or if I, for example, change this to a number, I'm now going to get an error as well right here. This is a number, it should be a Boolean. So this is where tuples are really easy to define because you essentially just create a hard-coded array of different types.

And then when you define your tuple, you just need to make sure you create the array and follow the actual type definition. This is useful in a lot of different places. If you've ever used React before, the use state hook, for example, returns a tuple where the first value is your state and the second value is the updater for your state function. Also, object.entries is another great example of where a tuple is being returned. And it may seem like a fairly niche thing, but there's a lot of different places where you need to either return an array or use an array in a specific way where you know the value of each key.

A ref must always be null ⁓ or an actual ref object when we pass it to an input. And you'll notice that this by default sets to undefined because we pass in nothing. You'll notice that it returns to us a ref object. And if we go to this, you can see that this is an immutable object. It is read only. If I try to set something, it's essentially saying, hey, this is read only, I cannot set it. So if you pass along null to a ref, you're telling React, hey, you what? You handle this ref for me. If you want to use a ref with an input component or an HTML element, you need to make sure that you pass along the generic for what the HTML element is, and then pass along null as the default value. Now all the different type narrowing that TypeScript can do is really incredible, but there's certain scenarios where TypeScript isn't able to quite do all the type narrowing we want it to do, so that's why it introduced this satisfies keyword. Take for example this code. We have a simple to-do type with a title string, due date, that could be a string or a date, and it is complete Boolean. Then down here I'm defining a to-do, but I'm not actually typing it as a to-do. Right now I'm just defining a to-do. I gave it a title, a due date, a complete property, and down here I'm just setting my date to the fourth date.

Now you will notice there is a problem with my code that's not being picked up. You can see I gave it a complete property instead of an is complete property, and TypeScript isn't picking up on this typo, and that's because this to do right here isn't actually typed as my to-do type. I need to manually tell TypeScript, okay, this is a to-do type. So I can come in here and say to do just like this. And now you can see I'm getting that error being picked up. So if I change this to isComplete, you can see that that removes the error and it fixes that problem for me, which is great.

But by doing this, I've actually run into another error. And the reason for that is down here, my due date now can be either a string or a date. Even though in reality, if we look at our code as a normal person, not as TypeScript, well, this to do, I manually set the due date to a date. So I know for a fact that this thing is a date. And before I actually typed this as a to-do, TypeScript knew that this was a date. As you can see, I have no errors. But as soon as I try to give it this to do type, since the due date can be a string or a date, TypeScript just takes the types from the type that you've defined.

And uses them instead of the types inside of my actual object. So to get around this problem, what we can do is instead of actually defining our type right here, we can instead after our object say that it satisfies a particular type. And by doing that, it keeps all of the specific types that we have here, such as our due date being a date, but it allows us to actually type it as something like a to-do. So it'll pick up on any typos we have. So here I can just say satisfies, and I'm going to say it satisfies size the to-do type. Now you can see.

That my due date is specifically only a date, and that's because here I specified it as particularly a date. When I hover over this to do, you can see that the type for this to do is a title that is a string. It's got a due date, which is a date, and an is complete, which is true. And if I were to spell this wrong by saying complete instead, you can see I'm now getting an error. And it's saying, hey, this needs to have an is complete property on it instead of complete. You can see right here. And that's because we're saying it satisfies this to do type, which means it has to meet the minimum requirements of this type.

Sneha Mehra (00:02:25)  
But it can be more specific. So in our case, the minimum requirement is a title, which is a string, which we have, a due date, which is either a string or a date. In our case, we have a due date, which is a date, and it is complete property, which is Boolean, and that's why we're getting our error, because this is spelled incorrectly. Now the reason this satisfies property is so nice is because it allows you to keep the more specific types that we've already defined, for example the fact our due date is a date, while making sure it actually follows the more generic type that we want it to.

So essentially satisfies just says my more specific thing must meet these minimum requirements. If it does, that's great, I'm not going to get any errors, and I'm going to still get to keep all my extra typing information that I had before. So a lot of times when you're defining an object like this that is supposed to be one type and you want to give it more specific information than the actual type gives you, that's where the satisfies property, which is relatively new to TypeScript, is really amazing.

Just ignore this line of code completely and do not do anything with it. So if you write a comment like this and you say at TS, you'll notice we have a couple different options.
